# Visnu-sahasra-nama (DB)

## Foreword

Although Srila Baladeva Vidyabhusana is particularly famous for his commentaries on the Vedanta-sutras and Bhagavad­gita, he also penned other lesser-known yet transcendentally important works, among them his Namartha-sudha commentary on Visnu-sahasra-nama. This English rendition is especially welcome to me, as over the last several years I have been giving a series of talks on each name, and Visnu-sahasra- nama has become a major part of my life. In preparing my talks, I refer to various commentaries, and I am gratified to now be able to consult a thorough, carefully prepared English exposition of Namartha-sudha, which hopefully will supersede a previous interpretation which was circulated in that name but unfortunately incorporated many discrepancies.

Several times after giving talks on these various names of Visnu, I have been questioned by devotee listeners expressing doubts about the relevance of Visnu-sahasra-nama to Gaudiya Vaisnavas, considering that one name of Sri Rama is equivalent to one thousand names of Visnu and that one name of Krsna is equivalent to three names of Rama— so why bother with Visnu- sahasra-nama? Is not Visnu-sahasra-nama in the Vaikuntha mood that Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu taught His followers to transcend? In reply, I cite that in Caitanya-caritamrta we find Nimai Pandita, our own Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, ordering His dear devotee Sri Srivasa Pandita to recite Visnu-sahasra- nama, which indicates that Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu expected or knew that Srivasa Pandita had memorised and could recite this sacred text and that He Himself wanted to hear it. I also cite that our Gaudiya acarya Srila Baladeva Vidyabhusana Prabhu wrote a commentary on Visnu-sahasra-nama, in which he reveals meanings of many names relevant to Krsna’s Vrndavana pastimes. Srila Bhaktivinoda Thakura later published Visnu- sahasra-nama along with Namartha-sudha, and he wrote that the two main jewels in the Maha-bharata are Bhagavad-gita and Visnu-sahasra-nama. Apart from that, I contend that by discussing the imports of these names, automatically the topics of SrimadBhagavatam, Ramayana, and other seminal Vaisnava scriptures are invoked in glorification of the Bhagavatta (“Godness”), sweetness, kindness, and beauty of the Supreme Lord Whom Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu worshipped as Krsna. And within my talks, I usually use the name Krsna, even more than Visnu, to highlight that glorification of Visnu is ultimately meant for Krsna; Visnu and Krsna are non-different, yet still Krsna is the fountainhead of all Visnu forms.

This edition of Visnu-sahasra-nama with the Namartha-sudha commentary is the latest contribution of Bhakta Demian (Dr. Demian Martins), who has made his life’s mission the translation and publication of the writings of Srila Baladeva Vidyabhusana. With extraordinary dedication, determination, and patience, he travels throughout the Indian subcontinent, searching out lost and obscure manuscripts which he then, employing his considerable mastery in Sanskrit, translates. The result of this focused effort is a profuse output of many of the works of Sri Vidyabhusana and other Gaudiya luminaries, making them available to the English-speaking devotional, literary, and philosophical public for the first time. Behind each publication lies tremendous effort, the extent of which is probably known only to himself, to Sri Vidyabhusana— whom he worships by his life’s work— and Antaryami Bhagavan Sri Krsna, Who constantly inspires, enlightens, and in other ways assists him in his noble efforts.

I pray that the readers of this text be enlightened with further realization of the glorious Supreme Personality of Godhead, Who has been praised in these verses by Sri Bhismacarya, Sri Vaisampayana, Sri Vyasadeva Bhagavan, and Sri Baladeva Vidyabhusana.

Om tat sat.  
Bhakti Vikasa Swami

## Introduction

## Preface

Among the immense Vedic scriptures, the sahasra-nama-stotra, a hymn consisting of a thousand names, is a genre on its own, lespite being usually part of a Purana or other major otext, uch as the Maha-bharata. Various Vedic devas and devts have one or more of such hymns devoted to them, whose recitation s usually part of the worship in their respective agamika chools. Different versions of Lord Visnu’s thousand names ire seen in the Padma Purana, Garuda Purana and Skanda 7urana. Yet none of these is even slightly as well-known as he Visnu-sahasra-nama-stotra found in the Anusasana Parva of the Maha-bharata, which is indeed so exalted that it is often eferred to as “the” sahasra-nama-stotra, just as the Bhagavad­gita is unmistakably referred to by the mere word gita, dthough there are countless gitas. Moreover, in spite of being a bremost Vaisnava text, just as the Bhagavad-gita is, the Visnu- ahasra-nama-stotra is widely recited throughout the world, lot only by Vaisnavas, but by people of a variety of religious ind philosophical affiliations— from the layman Hindu to the idvaitavadi scholars. For thousands of people, the recitation of this hymn is a daily routine through their whole life, either it home or in a temple. The prominence of this stotra is further vinced by the fact that even other religious groups felt the need о have their own versions of it. Thus, Jain scholars composed rarious hymns, such as the Arhan-ndma-sahasra-samuccaya a collection of a thousand names of Arhat) by Hemacandra 11th- 12th century AD), and the Jina-sahasra-nama-stava by ’andita Asadhara (13th century AD). Buddhists wrote similar vorks glorifying Buddha’s names. A modern example is the luddha-sahasra-namavali by S.N. Goenka, written in Pali. A ollection of a thousand names of Guru Nanak (1469-1539 ID), the first Sikh preceptor, is attributed to his son, Sricanda. nterestingly, the Guru Granth Sahib contains multiple hymns on the names of God, and page 1082 is dedicated to the names of Lord Visnu and His various avataras.

## Context

After slaying Duryodhana along with all his brothers and chief warriors, the Pandava brothers came out victorious in the Kuruksetra war. Nevertheless, rather than becoming jubilant, Yudhisthira, dharma personified, was feeling utterly despondent and judged himself accountable for the sins accrued from the killings of scores of friends and relatives, including his own sons, for the sole purpose of occupying the royal throne. By the end of the Stri Parva, it is narrated that, accompanied by the widows of the Kurus, the Pandavas had assembled at the bank of Ganga in order to offer oblations for the relatives who had breathed their last on the battlefield. It was at that moment that Queen Kunti, in tears, requested them, “You should also offer oblations for Kama, your eldest brother.” This unexpected revelation left the five brothers flabbergasted, and it was a devastating blow to Yudhisthira. Learning now that they had also slain their own eldest brother, his grief knew no bounds. He then resolved that instead of ruling the kingdom, he would renounce the world and lead a life of penance in the forest in order to atone for all his sinful deeds. Kunti, Draupadi, and each of his brothers tried to persuade him to stick to his duty as the sovereign, but to no avail. Narada Muni also addressed Yudhisthira with wise instructions, but he remained immovable. Vyasadeva then briefly spoke about the importance of the execution of one’s duties, which prompted Yudhisthira to ask him for an elaborate explanation on dharma, particularly for those in the royal order. The all-knowing sage Vyasa advised Yudhisthira that to be enlightened on the deep intricacies of dharma, he should approach grandsire Bhisma, who was fully conversant with all scriptures and whose wisdom was unparalleled in this world. However, this suggestion increased Yudhisthira’s distress even more, for it reminded him of the deceitful way they had attacked and defeated Bhisma by keeping Sikhandi in the front line, knowing that the latter had originally been born as a woman, and as such, Bhisma would never raise a weapon against him. By the incessant showers of arrows from both Arjuna and Sikhandi, Bhisma now laid on a bed of arrows. How could Yudhisthira face him again and ask for instruction on dharma?

Understanding Yudhisthira’s predicament, Lord Krsna directed him to abide by the words of Vyasadeva, and at last he acceded. The very next day, all of them departed from Hastinapura to the presence of Bhisma in Kuruksetra. Despite having arrows pierced throughout his body, he was still alive and fully conscious. On account of a boon received from his father, Santanu, Bhisma would die only at the moment he wished, so he was merely awaiting an auspicious astrological position to depart, to which fifty-six days were still left. In fact, that was just a plea to occasion the monumental instructions then imparted by Bhisma through the whole Santi Parva up to the end of the Anusdsana Parva, comprising about one quarter of the whole Maha-bharata. Covering in depth nearly every aspect of duty, morality, and religiosity, those many thousands of verses are tantamount to a complete Purdna in extension, and to multiple scriptures in their vastness of knowledge. Towards the conclusion of his speech, Bhisma culminates with the recitation of the thousand names of Lord Visnu in response to Yudhisthira’s question regarding what a person seeking liberation should utter.

This whole incident was also meant to reveal Bhisma’s unexcelled qualities, as Krsna Himself praised his matchless virtues and wisdom, and declared that no one else in the three worlds could baffle death in this way. Bhisma was so powerful that he rendered both Parasurama’s and Krsna’s words futile: the former when He promised to Amba that He would kill Bhlsma, but could not, and the latter when He promised not to take a weapon during the battle, but had to pick up a chariot wheel when Bhlsma fiercely attacked Arjuna. In his commentary on the Srimad-Bhagavatam ([1.9.46](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-1/9.46)), Visvanatha Cakravarti discloses Bhisma’s real identity: nitya-parsade bhisme vasohpravesat tasyaivadeha-tyago bhagavata darsitah, yavad-adhikaram avasthitir adhikarikanam iti nyayena tasyaivamsena vasutve ca sthitir bhagaval-loke praptis ca, “Since Bhisma is actually an eternal associate of the Lord, his death, as demonstrated by the Supreme Lord, pertains only to an amsa of Bhisma that entered one of the Vasus. That amsa of Bhisma was in the position of Vasu and attained the Supreme Lord’s abode, in conformity with the following principle: yavad-adhikaram avasthitir adhikarikanam (Brahma-sutra, 3.3.32), “Those in a position of authority remain in the universe so long as they have a duty.’ nitya-parsada-bhutasya bhismasya tv aprakata-lilayam partha-sarathi-praptir uktaiva, “Being an eternal associate of the Lord, it is said that Bhisma attained the Supreme Lord in His form as the charioteer of Arjuna in aprakata-ltta”

## Commentaries

Among the multiple sahasra-nama-stotras, the present one is by far the most commented on. Some attribute the great popularity of the Visnu-sahasra-nama-stotra to Saiikaracarya (7th- 8th century AD), who penned what is today known as its oldest commentary. Yet Sankara commented only on a few texts, such as the Brahma-sutra, Upanisads, and the Bhagavad-gita, which at that time were already established among learned circles as the authoritative basis of their philosophical propositions. On these grounds, it is plausible to conjecture that the Visnu-sahasra-nama-stotra was by then a similarly recognised text. Nothing discards the possibility that multiple commentaries may have existed in the pre-Sarikara period and later disappeared, as it happened to the various Brahma- sUtra commentaries whose existence is known only through secondary references. There is no doubt that Sankara’s commentaries have given impetus to subsequent scholars of different sampradayas to present their own explanations of the same texts as a means to both refute his views and establish their respective conclusions.

Parasara Bhatta (11 th-12th century AD), who succeeded Ramanujacarya as the abbot of the Sn-sampradaya, composed a commentary entitled Bhagavad-guna-darpana (a mirror of the Supreme Lord’s qualities), wherein he extensively elaborated on the transcendental and eternal attributes of the Lord of Vaikuntha, His kindness towards His devotees, and the glories of their unalloyed devotion for Him. This seems to be the oldest extant Vaisnava commentary, and it has been complemented by a sub-commentary named Nirvacana, attributed to Varadacarya, which clarifies Bhatta’s interpretations and exhaustively explains each name according to Panini’s system of grammar. In the form of verses in the anustup metre, a further commentary called Nirukti, whose authorship is unknown, summarises the meanings given by Bhatta.

Although Madhvacarya (1238-1317 AD) did not write a commentary on the sahasra-na ma per se, he often glossed Lord Visnu’s names throughout his various works. In the light of these, several generations of his followers presented a variety of commentaries. Vidyadhiraja Tirtha (14th century AD), a prominent disciple of Jaya Tirtha, composed a commentary called Visnu-sahasra-nama-nirukti. Vadiraja Tirtha (15th- 16th century AD) commented on selected names as part of the Maha-bhdrata-laksalankara, meant to gloss one hundred thousand important words through the whole Mahd-bharata. Raghimatha Tirtha (18th century AD) and Satyasandha Tirtha (ibid.) also penned notable commentaries. All of them were abbots in their respective mathas.

There are many other commentaries composed by Vaisnava followers of Ramanujacarya, Madhvacarya, Nimbarkacarya, and Visnu Svami, both ancient and modem, both in Sanskrit and vernacular, and there are dozens of commentaries written by authors non-affiliated to any Vaisnava school.

## Structure and Exegesis

For the average reader, Lord Visnu’s names here may seem to appear one after another at random, without any specific pattern or connection. It can be easily observed that sometimes names that begin with a particular letter are grouped together, but that is not consistent throughout the text. On the other hand, through their realisation, insight, and scholarship, Vaisnavacaryas have unveiled innumerable mysteries hidden within this text, and that is just a fraction of something that is actually unlimited. It is said:

*trayo’rthah sarva-vedesu dasdrthah sarva-bharate*  
*visnoh sahasra-namapi nirantara-satarthakam*

**“Each mantra in the four Vedas has at least three meanings; each verse in the Maha-bharata has at least ten meanings; and each of Lord Visnu’s thousand names has no less than a hundred meanings.”**

There is indeed no stringent principle to determine only one pattern to be followed while reading the names. Although in the traditional way, the commentators divide the whole text in ten cycles of a hundred names, the structure of the verses is flexible enough to accommodate the interpretation of some forty or fifty extra names, or even more if one would resort to everything that could be possibly justified by grammatical norms. The number comes down to an exact one thousand by either grouping words together as noun and adjective, or by reading them as compounds, as the case may be. The rules of external euphonic combination also add to an extra number of varieties. Moreover, there is a certain amount of alternative readings, and sometimes the commentaries offer different meanings depending on the reading that is chosen. Thus, each commentator has his particular way to look into the text and determine where a single name ends and the next one begins. Consequently, there is a mismatch in the list of names among various commentaries. Although only a thousand names appear in this hymn, Lord Visnu has unlimited names, but these thousand names can have unlimited meanings when interpreted in connection with thousands of forms and thousands of attributes. At the same time, these unlimited names and forms converge upon one and the same undivided Supreme Person, and are non-different from Him.

Many of Lord Visnu’s names are repeated several times in different verses, which some bigoted writers deride as a literary flaw called punarukti (tautology). Vaisnava scholars, however, have totally dismissed such shortsightedness by explaining each instance of the same name from a variety of distinct perspectives and interpretations. A similar accusation has been made on the occasional use of indeclinable words here, all of which are actually full of meaning, in contrast to what opponents allege to be mere empty words.

There are basically three ways in which norms can be interpreted: according to etymology (yoga), to convention (rddhi), and to both (yoga-rtidha). The etymological meaning of the word karta is “doer,” since it is directly formed from the verbal root kr (to do). Literally, ratha-kara means “chariot­maker,” but conventionally this is the name of a caste. The word pankaja etymologically means “born in the mud,” and conventionally it means “lotus.” As the lotus is born in the mud, both senses are correctly applied here. Conventional meanings can be twofold: ordinary and those given by learned scholars (vidvad-rfldhi). Etymology is also twofold: according to grammar (vaiyakarani), and according to the usage of the sages (arsi). Furthermore, the common etymology applied to names of demigods and so on is here applied to the Supreme Lord in their meta-primary meaning by meta-etymology (maha-yoga). By means of all these, Vaisnava commentators not only shed light on each of the Lord’s names individually, but also establish contextual connections between groups of names, interpreting them in relation to various avataras and contemplating Their respective pastimes while reading through those names.

## Importance

Both the sruti and the smrti abound in statements glorifying the Holy Names of Lord Hari as the utmost means to attain the ultimate goal of life, particularly in the Age of Kali. The glories of the Holy Names are found throughout the Maha-bharata, which is considered the fifth Veda. The Maha-bharata has two crest jewels: the Gita delivered by Lord Krsna immediately before the battle, and the thousand names delivered by Bhisma soon after the battle ended. The excellence of the Visnu-sahasra- nama-stotra has been particularly praised in the Purarias in the following words:

*sastresu bharatam saram tatra ndma-sahasrakam*  
*vaisnavam krsna-glta ca taj-jnanan mucyate’njasa*

**“The Maha-bharata is the essence of all scriptures, and the essence of the Maha-bharata is the thousand names of Visnu and the Gita spoken by Krsna. By knowing both of these, one quickly becomes liberated.”**

*bharatam sarva-sastresu bharate gitika vara*  
*visnoh sahasra-namapi jneyam pathyam ca tad dvayam*

**“Ihe Maha-bharata is the best among all scriptures, and within the Maha-bharata, the Bhagavad-gita and the Visnu-sahasra- nama are the best parts. Both should be recited and understood.”**

*visnor ekaika-namapi sarva-vedadhikam matam*  
*tebhyas cananta-ndmabhyo’dhikam namnam sahasrakam*

**“Every single name of Lord Visnu is considered superior to all the Vedas, and among His unlimited names, the Visnu-sahasra- nama is superior.” (Skanda Purana, Vaisnava-kharida, 21.53)**

In the introduction of his commentary, Parasara Bhatta summarises the importance of the Visnu-sahasra-nama-stotra into six points in the following words:

*mahabharata-saratvad rsibhih pariganatah*  
*vedacarya-samaharad bhismotkrsta-matatvatah*  
*parigrahatisayato gitady-aikarthyatas ca nah*  
*sahasra-nanmam adhyaya upadeyatamo matah*

**“The chapter with the thousand names of Lord Visnu is considered by us as the most excellent because 1) it is the essence of the Maha-bharatcr, 2) it is sung by great sages; 3) it is has been compiled by Vedacarya Vyasadeva; 4) it presents Bhisma’s highest views; 5) it is exceedingly revered; and 6) it holds the same meaning as the Bhagavad-gita and other scriptures.”**

As the Maha-bharata itself is the essence of all scriptures, and the Visnu-sahasra-nama is the essence of the Maha-bharata, it is understood that the Visnu-sahasra-nama is ultimately the quintessence of all scriptures. Its endorsement by great sages such as Narada further corroborates its relevance and exalted position among the scriptures. Having being compiled by Vyasadeva, Who is Lord Narayana Himself, its authority is beyond any doubt. In the chapter of the thousand names of Visnu, Bhisma presents his final conclusion about the multiple topics that had been previously discussed for so many days. From time immemorial, the Visnu-sahasra-nama has been given utmost respect by sadhus, scholars, and religious people in general, and by presenting Visnu as the Supreme Lord of all and His names as the best means of liberation, it is in full consonance with the whole sruti and smrti. Moreover, by the addition of the pranava and the word namah to each of Lord Visnu’s names, a thousand different mantras are formed, which hold the same status as the mantras in the Vedas.

## Philosophy in the Thousand Names

More than merely bringing out various meanings of the names and the connection between them, commentators found in the Visnu-sahasra-nama a powerful vehicle to impart their philosophical conclusions, and thus their interpretations are often interspersed with the views of their respective systems and doctrines. To add more authority to their comments, they also adopted the traditional dialectical approach of raising possible objections (purva-paksa) to their propositions and refuting them, amply quoting from the Upanisads, Puranas, Brahma-sutra, and other scriptures to corroborate their arguments. This being the case in other philosophical schools, Vaisnavas feel naturally inclined to gloss this text, wherein Vaisnava siddhanta distinctly flows. Lord Visnu, or Krsna, is the central topic of the Maha-bharata amidst innumerable other topics, but in the thousand names chapter, He is the sole topic, without any scope for diversions. Bhisma’s answers to Yudhisthira’s questions are thus crystal clear: Visnu is the Supreme Being, the God of gods, and His names are the means to liberation. In fact, without distorting the literal meaning of the text and applying a good amount of imagination, it is not possible to come to any understanding apart from this.

Material objects and their names have different natures and attributes. The mere utterance of the word “water” serves no purpose to a thirsty person, for it is just a vibration of two syllables in the air, while the substance, water, consists of molecules of hydrogen and oxygen. This principle does not apply to God and His names, for neither is a combination of material elements. The scriptures define sabda-brahma, spiritual sound, as another aspect of the Supreme Absolute Truth, which is eternal, transcendental, and immutable. Each of Lord Visnu’s names expresses His forms, qualities, and pastimes, and there is no limit in what can be expressed by a single name. The sruti, the smrti, and the names of God are non-different from God Himself, sharing the same spiritual attributes:

*nama cintamanih krsnas*  
*caitanya-rasa-vigrahah*  
*purnah suddho nitya-mukto*  
*’bhinnatvan nama-naminoh*

**“The Holy Name is Krsna Himself, a philosopher’s stone, a form of consciousness and rasa. It is complete, pure, and eternally free from the illusory energy, for there is no difference between the name and the named.”**

Ramanujacarya defines this relation between the name and the referent, or the quality and the qualified, in terms of aprthak-siddhi, while Madhvacarya explains their apparent difference on the basis of visesa. Following in their footsteps, subsequent acaryas explained the thousand names in the light of these philosophical views. One of the key points in Vaisnava thought, in clear contrast with other systems, is that Lord Visnu’s names are not only a means to attain liberation— they are also the goad, Lord Visnu Himself. Reaching this goal involves the attainment of one’s svartipa in the spiritual world, where one eternally maintains an identity different from that of God, although sharing similar spiritual attributes. Iherein, one remains serving the Lord in various capacities, including the continuous recitation of His names.

## Application

The great popularity of the Visnu-sahasra-nama-stotra as an integral part of religious practises around the world is significantly due to its easiness and lack of requirements or restrictions. In contrast to the hymns of the four Vedas, which in most traditional circles are recited only by male brahmanas who properly learnt Vedic intonation within a particular branch (sakha), the sahasra-nama can be recited by anyone without any considerations of gender, caste, qualification, time, place, or purity. Contrary to Vedanta philosophy, which is very complex and takes years of study to be grasped, the sahasra- nama can be promptly recited by common people, whether they understand its meanings or not. Contrary to Vedic sacrifices, which require substantial amounts of money, paraphernalia, and qualified priests, the sahasra-nama requires none of these.

Even those who are illiterate or unable to pronounce Sanskrit correctly can still benefit by hearing others reciting Lord Visnu’s names. Moreover, there are multiple ways in which one can benefit from the sahasra-nama: by reading mentally, by reading aloud, by singing, by hearing, by writing down, by memorising, by remembering, by meditating on the names, by meditating on the meanings, by propagating its glories, by arranging a recitation, by gifting it to others, etc.

In order to encourage those who are afflicted or materially motivated, the thousand names are followed by a list of results that may accrue from their recitation: freedom from inauspiciousness, victory, wealth, happiness, dharma, sense enjoyment, progeny, fame, prominence, heroism, power, freedom from fear, freedom from disease, effulgence, strength, beauty, good qualities, and freedom from sin. Indeed, the sahasra-nama is well-known as a panacea for all sorts of miseries, and it has been applied as such in traditional medicine and astrology for centuries. In the Brhat-parasara-hora-sastra, an ancient treatise on jyotisa attributed to the sage Parasara, its recitation is repeatedly recommended to counteract ailments caused by planetary influence. For instance:

*visnu-nama-sahasram ca hy anna-danam ca karayet*  
*rajata-pratima-danam kuryad arogya-siddhaye*

**“In order to become free from disease, one should recite the Visnu-sahasra-nama, give grains in charity, and present a deity made of silver as a gift.” (Brhat-parasara-hora-sastra, 54.57)**

*tad-dosa-pariharartham visnu-sahasrakam japet*  
*ayur-vrddhi-karam caiva sarva-saubhagya-dayakam*

**“In order to counteract such ill-effects, one should recite Lord Visnu’s thousand names, which yield longevity and all good fortune.” (Ibid., 58.31)**

In the Caraka-samhita (3.312), a standard text on Ayurvedic medicine attributed to Caraka (c. 1st century BC), it is said:

*visnum sahasra-murdhanarh caracara-patim vibhum*  
*stuvan nama-sahasrena jvaran sarvan apohati*

**“By glorifying the thousand-headed Lord Visnu, the almighty Lord of all moving and non-moving beings, with His thousand names, one heals all sorts of fevers.”**

Subsequently, Bhisma mentions a few more results that may be particularly appealing to those seeking spiritual advancement: understanding the conclusion of the Vedas, self­realisation, tolerance, freedom from desires, self-restraint, and remembrance of Lord Vasudeva. In fact, for those devotees who recite Lord Visnu’s names with the sole purpose of pleasing Him, the foremost result is increasing and unflinching devotion to Him.

## Namartha-sudha

The context in which Baladeva Vidyabhusana (c. 1700-1793 AD) composed this commentary seems to be the same that led him to comment on the Brahma-sutra, the Upanisads, and the Bhagavad-gita: the necessity to legitimise the Gaudiyas as a bona fide Vaisnava-sampradaya when their credibility had been questioned in the court of King Sawai Jai Singh П (ruled 1699-1743 AD) in Amber. By substantiating the affiliation to the Madhva-sampradaya and proving that the Gaudiyas hold valid interpretations of the prasthana-traya, Vidyabhusana succeeded in demonstrating their legitimacy. Yet the mission was not over, for the Gaudiyas still lacked their own versions of other texts that consolidated the status acquired by prominent religious groups such as the Sankara-sampradaya and the Sri- sampradaya. Although not being primarily of philosophical character, the Visnu-sahasra-nama-stotra definitely already occupied a distinct position among scholars and religious leaders, and it is likely that Vidyabhusana envisioned this commentary to corroborate the authoritativeness of the Gaudiya line and to propagate its tenets. The date of the composition is unknown, but it was probably written long after the demise of Jai Singh, and possibly after other major commentaries. This is corroborated on name 889, where we see a verse written by Vedantavagisa in his Suksma-tika on Govinda-bhasya (4.3.4).

Although the author does not directly acknowledge the names of other commentators, he often refers to their interpretations, which indicates that he had access to multiple commentaries. While some of the meanings, explanations, and quotes are clearly coming from Sankara, Bhatta, and Varadacarya, there are several instances in which his sources could not be traced in the nearly fifteen ancient Sanskrit commentaries consulted in preparing this edition, some of which are unpublished manuscripts. Even while taking clues from previous commentaries, Vidyabhusana made the Namartha-sudha an original and unprecedented work in many respects, managing to extract a Gaudiya flavour out of the text in a unique way. Instead of the common trend shown by other authors in interpreting the names in connection with Narayana, the Lord of Vaikuntha, and the aisvarya mood, here a large number of names has been attributed to Lord Krsna in connection with His pastimes in Vrndavana, Mathura, and Dvaraka. The whole text has been divided by Vidyabhusana into sections of names attributed to various avataras and their respective pastimes, and the connections and meanings are presented in a very personal and innovative manner. In several places, he remarks that Visnu is actually an expansion of Krsna, the source of all avataras, and also corroborates Sri Radha’s position as the source of all Laksmis. Throughout the commentary, the author substantiates many of his interpretations by quoting extensively from the Upanisads, Puranas, Bhagavad-glta, Maha­bharata, Tantras, etc. Rather than being directly from their primary sources, some of these scriptural quotes are actually from the works of Madhvacarya, Rupa Gosvami, Sanatana Gosvami, and Jiva Gosvami, which also reveals how deeply Vidyabhusana had studied their works. As an accomplished grammarian, Vidyabhusana also occasionally disagrees with other commentators regarding how certain names are formed and based on which rules. He continuously refers to Panini’s grammar to explain both the formation and meaning of names.

## Sources

The following editions and manuscripts have been consulted in this critical edition and translation:

ka) Text in Bengali characters with partial Bengali translation by Vipina Vihari Gosvami, published by Kedaranatha Datta, Gaurabda 400 (1886 AD), Kolkata. The same was published again by the Sri Caitanya Asrama, Kharagpur, in 1974.

kha) Text in Devanagari with partial Hindi translation by Krsnadasa Baba, Samvat 2023 (1966 AD), Kusuma Sarovara.

ga) Manuscript in Devanagari, preserved at the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute in Pune, accession number 181 of 1887-91, complete, Samvat 1860 (1803 AD), copied by Kisoridasa from a manuscript that belonged to Krsnadasa, Jaipur.

gha) Manuscript in Devanagari, preserved at the Nimbarka Sanskrit Mahavidyalaya in Vrindavan, accession number 6430, complete, Samvat 1892 (1835 AD).

na) Manuscript in Devanagari, preserved at the Vrindavan Research Institute, accession number 7013, complete, undated. It belonged to Atula Krsna Gosvami.

ca) Manuscript in Bengali characters, preserved at the Vrindavan Research Institute, accession number 18261, incomplete, undated. It belonged to Madhava Ramanuja Dasa Jeeyar.

cha) Manuscript in Devanagari, preserved at M. G. Kashi Vidyapith, accession number 942, complete, Samvat 1878 (AD 1821), copied by Lalitadasa.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura is to be lauded for publishing the text for the first time, but the Sanskrit was poorly revised, and besides numerous mistakes, omissions also happened. No mention is made as to the manuscript consulted. In his preface, Vipina Vihari states that he was not going to translate the whole text due to the apprehension that the book may become too voluminous. Ironically, he did not hesitate to give his own glosses in the middle of the commentary, totally ignoring those by Vidyabhusana. Quite often, his translations are not at all related to what the commentator actually wrote. Despite the claim that the book was thoroughly revised and corrected, the reprint by the Sri Caitanya Asrama has the same shortcomings of the first edition. Surprisingly, they removed Vipina Vihari Gosvami’s preface and name from the book, and instead added Bhaktivinoda’s name as the translator. They also removed Bhaktivinoda’s foreword, in which he acknowledges his guru’s translation.

Edition ‘kha’ is an exact transcription of the first edition, without improvements or corrections. The Hindi translation is also totally based on the Bengali translation, which gives the impression that the translator did not even look at the Sanskrit text.

Manuscript ‘ga’ has spelling mistakes and other lapses, but it was very helpful to ascertain correct readings. Manuscript ‘gha’ is more accurate than the above one but is far from being flawless. It contains a few corrections here and there. Manuscript ‘na’ is the oldest one, possibly from the author’s days. It has the most accurate readings but also multiple errors. Manuscript ca’has some odd readings and various side notes, some irrelevant and some that were subsequently interpolated in the main text. Since it was written on machine paper, it cannot be older than the late 19th century, and hence those notes must have been copied as well. Manuscript ‘cha has some correct readings in spots where other manuscripts were incorrect, but it also shares several common errors.

It is apparent from the above manuscripts that there should be at least a few more manuscript copies, which unfortunately could not be located yet, supposing they still exist. There being thousands of Visnu-sahasra-nama manuscripts all over India and abroad, if the commentator’s name is not mentioned in the respective lists and catalogues, as it is often the case, the time and effort required to find a particular commentary is beyond a single person’s capacity.

For reference purposes, Sanskrit commentaries by the following authors have been consulted: Sarikaracarya, Parasara Bhatta, Varadacarya, Vidyadhiraja Tirtha, Vadiraja Tirtha, Raghunatha Tirtha, Satyasandha Tirtha, Srivallabha (unpublished), Kurmanrsiriihacarya (unpublished), Balabhadra (unpublished), Pangari Srinivasacarya, Sukadeva Sudhi, Mahadeva Vedanti (unpublished), Bannanje Govindacarya, and a few unknown ones.

In the present edition, an effort has been made in order to render the English translation as close as possible to the grammatical structure seen in the original. The idea is to help the readers to understand how the names are actually explained in Sanskrit, in a way of expression close to that of the commentary. Naturally, there were several instances in which a strict literal translation was either not possible or would imply a very awkward sentence in English. In such cases, equivalents have been given with a more flowing construction. The translations of Panini’s siitras here are not meant to be exhaustive but are mostly focused on the specific grammatical principles that apply to a certain name. In their original context, they often imply multiple principles from preceding aphorisms, and those who want a thorough explanation may directly consult Panini and his commentators. Vidyabhusana has been very particular about the readings of the verses he has quoted from the Bhagavad-gita, Srimad-Bhagavatam, and so on. These have been translated primarily according to his own comments here, as well as the context. Additionally, the interpretation of some of those verses follows the commentaries that Vidyabhusana, Visvanatha Cakravarti, Sridhara Svami, and other acaryas wrote on those scriptures.

Both in the index and in the translation here, most names appear in the nominative form minus the visarga (h), if any. Although this pattern may be arbitrary from the grammatical point of view, it portrays closely the name forms as they appear in Indian vernaculars. When a name is accompanied by an adjective, or is in coordination with one or more words, the original case endings have been retained. Although hyphens are normally not used within a proper noun, they have been maintained here for the sake of facilitating the comprehension of how the names are formed according to the given interpretation. This applies mostly in instances where each part of the noun is an independent word, and not in case of verbal stems, affixes, etc. Capitals have been given only for the first word, although it may be an adjective qualifying the actual name. The order of the adjectives and names has been changed only when that has been done in the commentary. Whenever the author does not gloss words whose primary meaning is obvious to anyone who knows Sanskrit, they have been translated either between brackets or in the footnotes. There were a few places in which none of the consulted manuscripts had reliable readings. These have been duly explained in the footnotes. Valid alternative readings are few and most do not affect the interpretation of the text, and hence they have been omitted here. A digital version with all critical notes will be made available subsequently.

## Visnu-sahasra-nama-stotram

### Text 1

*vaisampayana uvaca*  
*srutva dharman asesena pavanani ca sarvasah*  
*yudhisthirah santanavam punar evabhyabhasat*

**Перевод:**

**Vaisampayana said: Having fully heard about various kinds of dharma and all purifying means, Yudhisthira again enquired from Bhisma, the son of King Santanu.**

**Комментарий:**

*ananta-kalyana-gunaika-varidhir*  
*vibhus cid-ananda-ghano bhajat-priyah*  
*krsnas tri-saktir bahu-miirtir isvaro*  
*visvaika-hetuh sa karotu nah subham \|\|1\|\|*

**Krsna, the All-powerful Lord, is a unique ocean of unlimited auspicious qualities, the embodiment of consciousness and bliss, and the beloved of those who worship Him. He is the supreme controller, possesses three potencies, has multiple forms, and is the sole cause of the universe. May He bring us good fortune.**

*vyasam satyavati-sutarii muni-gurum narayanarn samstumo*  
*vaisampayanam acyutahvaya-sudha-modam prapadyamahe*  
*gangeyarn mura-mardana-priyatamam sarvartha-samvid-varam*  
*tat-sabhyan api tat-katha-rasa-juso bhuyo namaskurmahe \|\|2\|\|*

**I glorify the son of Satyavati, Sri Vyasadeva, who is the guru of all sages and is Lord Narayana Himself. I take shelter of the sage Vaisampayana, whose joy is the nectarean names of Lord Acyuta. I repeatedly offer obeisances to the son of Gangadevi, Bhismadeva, who has the most excellent knowledge of the import of all scriptures and is very dear to Lord Murari; and also to those who were assembled around him, relishing the taste of his narrations about the Lord.**

*nityam nivasatu hrdaye caitanyatma murarir nah*  
*niravadyo nirvrtiman gajapatir anukampaya yasya \|\|3\|\|*

First interpretation

**May Sri Rasikananda Murari, whose heart was fully fixed on Lord Caitanya, always reside within our hearts. By his mercy, Gopala dasa, the king of elephants, gave up his animal nature and became ecstatic upon serving the devotees of the Lord.**

Second interpretation

**May Lord Murari in the form of Caitanya Mahaprabhu eternally reside within our hearts. By His mercy, Gajapati Prataparudra was freed from all worldly passions and became absorbed in the happiness of love for Krsna.**

Third interpretation

**May Lord Murari, Whose transcendental form is pure consciousness, eternally reside within our hearts. By His mercy, Gajendra, the king of elephants, gave up his animal body and attained liberation.**

*yugapan navyanandan prakirtayan yani vindate sesah*  
*kartrny abhista-purter jayanti namani tani krsnasya \|\|4\|\|*

**All glories to Lord Krsna’s Holy Names, which satisfy all desires. Singing them, Ananta Sesa enjoys newer and newer bliss all together.**

*ye namaika-pumartha hari-bhaktas tan namasyamah*  
*bhasyam yesaih krpaya namartha-sudha bhaved etat \|\|5\|\|*

**I offer obeisances unto those devotees of Lord Hari whose sole goal of life is His Holy Names and by whose mercy this commentary entitled Namartha-sudha will come into existence.**

Vaisampayana’s greatness is declared in the Puranas:

*sampam satahradam ahur ayanam patanarh tatah*  
*vigatam sabdato yasmat tad vaisampayanah smrtah*

**“The wise say that sampa means ‘thunderbolt’ and ayana means ‘fall.’ Because he was bom from the sound of a thunderbolt, this sage is named Vaisampayana.”**

The sage Vaisampayana was a dear disciple of Vyasadeva. Although not asked about the hymn that will be now recited, Vaisampayana spoke the above words to King Janamejaya out of affection and kindness, as well as due to the supreme excellence of this hymn. After having fully (asesatah) heard (srutva) about various kinds of dharma, such as that pertaining to the royal order, and also about all (sarvasah) purifying means (pavanani), such as holy places of pilgrimage, King Yudhisthira once again (punah) enquired (abhyabhasata) from Bhisma (santavanam).

### Text 2

*yudhisthira uvaca*  
*kim ekam daivatam loke kim vapy ekam parayanam*  
*stuvantah kam kam arcantah prapnuyur manavah subham*

**Перевод:**

**Whom do you consider the chief God in the Vedus? What do you consider the chief shelter of all? Glorifying whom and worshipping whom do people attain good fortune?**

**Комментарий:**

“Bhisma has studied all the Upanisads and Vedas along with their subsidiary treatises, and he is endowed with all religious observances, such as celibacy. On account of a boon given by the Supreme Lord, he is free from pain caused by weapons, and having become omniscient, he can see the lower and higher truth just as one sees a gooseberry in the palm of the hand. Therefore, the Supreme Absolute Truth should be inquired into. Being questioned about it in a general way, he will speak about it,” — thinking in this way, Yudhisthira asks the above questions. The word loke (in the sacred scriptures) in this verse and the words bhavatah paramo matah (considered the best by you) in the next verse are connected with every question. The case endings should be changed as required. The word loke means in the Vedas and other sacred scriptures, as per the following etymological definition: lokyate tattvam aneneti, “that by which the truth is seen (lokyate).” “Whom (kim) do you consider the chief (ekam) God (daivatam)?” The second question is: “Which thing (kim) do you consider the chief (ekam) shelter of all (parayanam)?” The third and fourth questions are as follows: “Who is that God, glorifying (stuvantah) Whom (kam), and worshipping (arcantah) Whom (kam) people (manavah) would attain (prapnuyuh) good fortune (subham)?” In other words, “according to your opinion, by worshipping whom and by glorifying whose qualities and activities can one attain prosperity and liberation?”

### Text 3

*ко dharmah sarva-dharmanam bhavatah paramo matah*  
*kim japan mucyate jantur janma-samsara-bandhanat*

**Перевод:**

**Among all dharmas, which one do you consider the best? By uttering what can the individual soul become free from the bondage of birth and worldly life?**

**Комментарий:**

The fifth question is this: “Among all (sarva) dharmas (dharmanam) that you have spoken, which one (kah) is considered (matah) by you (bhavatah), who are omniscient, as the best one (paramah), able to yield the desired results without exertion?” The sixth question is this: “According to your opinion, by uttering (japan) what (kim) can the individual soul (jantuh) become free (mucyate) from the bondage (bandhanat) of birth (janma) and worldly life (samsara)?” Birth, characterised by falling into a hell-like womb, is the effect. Worldly life, characterised by previous material activities, is the cause. The bondage that results from birth and worldly life prevents the attainment of the supreme abode. It is from this bondage that one becomes liberated.

### Text 4

*sri-bhisma uvaca*  
*jagat-prabhum deva-devam anantam purusottamam*  
*stuvan nama-sahasrena purusah satatotthitah*

**Перевод:**

**Bhisma said: A person who desires to attain the goal of life is always engaged in glorifying the Lord of the universe, the worshipable God of all gods, the unlimited Supreme Person, by His thousand names.**

**Комментарий:**

Having been thus questioned about six things in a general way, in the first half of this verse the greatest scholar Bhisma gives a specific answer to the first question. The Lord (prabhum) of the movable and unmovable universe (jagat) is the worshipable God of all gods (deva-devam), and by His Own nature, qualities, and potencies, He is unlimited (anantam). Therefore, He is the highest being among all beings (purusottamam), both in the states of bondage and liberation. Leaving aside the order of the questions on account of His eagerness for this topic, Bhisma replies to the third question in the second half of this verse: A person who desires to attain the goal of life (purusah) is always (satata) engaged (utthitah) in glorifying (stuvan) the Supreme Person by His thousand names, which will now be recited, and also in reciting His qualities and activities— this is also the answer to the sixth question. This sentence is connected with the words ‘sarva-duhkhatigo bhavet’ (one crosses beyond all distress) in verse six.

### Text 5

*tam eva carcayan nityam bhaktya purusam avyayam*  
*dhyayan stuvan namasyams ca yajamanas tarn eva ca*

**Перевод:**

**Full of faith, such a worshipper constantly engages in worshipping only Krsna, the imperishable Supreme Person, and also in meditating on Him, glorifying Him, and offering obeisances unto Him.**

**Комментарий:**

Here Bhisma replies to the fourth question. The word yajamanah means “worshipper,” and bhaktya means “with faith.” Glorifying (stuvan) only (eva) Krsna, the Supreme Person (purusam), one crosses beyond all distress (sarva- duhkhatigo bhavet)— this is the connection between the words of this verse and the next. Glorifying in which way? Bhisma says: By worshipping (arcayan) Him with various articles, such as water for washing the feet; by meditating (dhyayan), that means by fixing the mind on His form; and by offering obeisances (namasyan) with eight limbs. He then specifies the Supreme Person as imperishable (avyayam), which means His svarUpa, qualities, potencies, and pastimes are all eternal. There is no fault in the double use of the word tam, since there is a different connection of words here.

### Text 6

*anadi-nidhanam visnum sarva-loka-mahesvaram*  
*lokadhyaksam stuvan nityam sarva-duhkhatigo bhavet*

**Перевод:**

**Constantly glorifying the all-pervasive Lord Visnu, the supreme controller of all planetary systems, Who is devoid of birth and death and is the observer of all planets and their rulers, one crosses beyond all distress.**

**Комментарий:**

While speaking about the result, since this is useful in the contextual purpose (prakrtopayogi) here, Bhisma specifies the Supreme Person in this and the next verse. The words anadi- nidhanam mean “devoid of birth and death, eternal.” This is because He is all-pervasive (visnum) and the supreme controller (mahesvaram) of all (sarva) planetary systems (Ioka) and their rulers. The planetary systems and their rulers (Ioka) are under His observation (adhyaksam), just like a gooseberry is seen in the palm of the hand. Worshipping and glorifying Lord Visnu is appropriate, for His being a benefactor is indicated here by the words anadi-nidhanam: He is not limited by time, and also by the word visnum: He is not limited by place; His fulfilling all desires of the worshipper is indicated by the words sarva-loka-mahesvaram: He is the supreme controller of all planetary systems; and His omniscience is indicated by the compound lokadhyaksam: He is the observer of all planets and their rulers. Glorifying (stuvan) such a Supreme Person, one crosses beyond (atigah) all distress (sarva-duhkha). After having crossed beyond the conflagration in the form of threefold miseries and having then attained Lord Krsna, Who is an ocean of bliss, one becomes blissful. That means one becomes liberated. In the first verse spoken by Bhlsma, the mere utterance of the Holy Names is stated; in the second verse, their recitation accompanied by the Lord’s worship and so on; and in this verse, their utterance in whatever manner, as corroborated by statements such as:

*sakrd uccaritam yena harir ity aksara-dvayam*  
*baddhah parikaras tena moksaya gamanam prati*  
*(Padma Purana, 6.80.161)*

**“For one who even once utters these two syllables, На-ri, the means to quickly attaining liberation is already effected by this.”**

*sakrd api parigltarh sraddhaya helaya va*  
*bhrgu-vara nara-matram tarayet krsna-nama*

**“O best of the Bhrgus (Saunaka), the Holy Name of Krsna delivers any person who has uttered it even once, either with faith or carelessly.”**

The scriptures declare that by the Lord’s name, in which merely the speech is engaged, one attains liberation, which is obtained with difficulty by performing pilgrimage to holy places, austerities, and meditation continuously and zealously for a long time. As for the Vedic scholars who are ignorant of the power of the Holy Name and say that such scriptural statements are an exaggerated praise (arthavada), they are actually brutes. Otherwise, the following statement of the smrti would be contradicted: tatharthavado hari-namni kalpanam (Padma Purana, 4.25.16), “It is an offence to consider the glories of Lord Hari’s name as an exaggerated praise or imagination.”

### Text 7

*brahmanyam sarva-dharma-jnam lokanaih kirti-vardhanam*  
*loka-natham mahad bhutam sarva-bhuta-bhavodbhavam*

**Перевод:**

**He is the protector of the brahmanas and the knower of all kinds of dharma. He increases the glories of people who perform dharma. He is the ruler of all inhabitants of the universe and is worshipable to them. He already existed before all of them were born, and He is the source of Brahma and Siva, who are themselves the origin of all created beings.**

**Комментарий:**

Lord Visnu is the benefactor of the brdhmanas’ Vedic knowledge and austerities, that is to say, He is their protector (brahmanyam). He knows (jnam) all kinds (sarva) of dharma spoken ofin the Vedas, such as Agnihotra and Darsapaurnamasa, as well as His Own worship, meditation, salutations, praises, and so on. Ihe meaning is that He is the giver of the results spoken of in the Vedas. He increases (vardhanam) the glories (kirti) of people (lokanam) who perform their respective dharma as described in the Vedas by accordingly giving them goods, knowledge, and detachment. He is the one to be petitioned (natham) by all inhabitants (Ioka) of the universe, starting with Brahma and Rudra, for the attainment of their desires. Or else, He is the master and rules (natham) over all inhabitants (Ioka) of the universe. The Paninlya Dhatu-patha (6-7) says: nathr nadhr yacnopatapaisvaryasihsu, “The verbal roots nath\[r\] and nadh\[r\] are used in the sense of requesting, harassing, ruling, and wishing.” As stated in the Maha-bharata: yuga-koti-sahasrani visnum aradhya padmabhuh, punas trailokya-dhatrtvam praptavan iti susruma, “We heard that after worshipping Lord Visnu for millions of yugas, Brahma then attained the status of creator of the three worlds.” mahadevah sarvamedhe mahatma hutvatmanam deva-devo babhuva, visvaml lokan vyapya vistabhya kirtya virajate dyutiman krtti-vasah (12.20.12), “After offering himself as an oblation in a great universal sacrifice, the magnanimous Lord Siva became the lord of the demigods. Pervading and supporting the whole universe, resplendent, and dressed in a skin, he now shines with glory.” etau tau vibudha-sresthau prasada-krodhajav ubhau, tad-adarsita-panthanau srsti- sariihara-karakau (12.328.16), “Brahma and Rudra, the best among demigods, are said to have been respectively born from Lord Narayana’s grace and anger, and they perform the creation and destruction according to the directions given by Him.” The same is also elaborated in other places.

Lord Visnu is worshipable (mahat) for all the inhabitants of the universe because He was already existing (bhutam) before they were born. Bhisma then describes this— Lord Visnu is the source (udbhavam) from which Brahma and Rudra are bom, who are themselves the origin (bhava) of all (sarva) created beings (bhuta). As stated in the Maha-bharata: prajapatim ca rudrarh capy aham eva srjami vai, tau hi mam na vijanito mama maya-vimohitau, “I alone create Prajapati Brahma and Rudra. Being bewildered by My illusory energy, neither of them knows Me.”

### Text 8

*esa me sarva-dharmanam dharmo’dhikatamo matah*  
*yad bhaktya pundarikaksam stavair arcen narah sada*

**Перевод:**

**I consider this the best among all kinds of dharma: a person should always worship the lotus-eyed Lord Krsna with words of praise and with full devotion.**

**Комментарий:**

Here Bhisma gives the answer to the fifth question. “Among all kinds (sarva) of dharma that I have spoken, this (esah) dharma is considered (matah) by me, Bhisma, as the best (adhikatamah).”

If someone asks what that dharma is, he replies in the next line. “A person (narah) should worship (arcet) the lotus-eyed (pundarikaksam) Lord Krsna with full devotion (bhaktya) by words of praise (stavaih) characterised by uttering His names, which are of His very nature.” The meaning is that worship in the form of recitation of Lord Krsna’s names with full faith is considered by Bhisma as the supreme dharma. Here, by the word bhaktya, this dharma is understood as superior to the worship of other deities, which is unpalatable like an astringent beverage; by the word pundarikaksam, it is understood as superior to the hymns of praise to other deities, who are disfigured and terrifying; and by the word stavaih, it is understood as superior to rituals that are performed with great effort, such as the Jyotistoma sacrifice. By the word narah, it is indicated that any restriction that one must be a twice- born and so on is ruled out, and by the use of the singular, the necessity of many assistants is ruled out. By the word sada (always), it is indicated that the requirements of place, time, and purity are ruled out. According to some, the word bhaktya means “by considering Krsna as the Lord, different from oneself.” It should be understood that the superexcellence of the Holy Names, as spoken by Bhisma, is such on account of their being a form of the Supreme Lord Himself. As stated in the sruti: orii, asya jananto nama cid vivaktana mahas te visno sumatim bhajamahe, om tat sat (Rg Veda, 1.156.3), “Om. О Lord Visnu, Your name is spiritual consciousness and is self- effulgent. Knowing even a little about Your name, by reciting it we attain spiritual knowledge. Om, that name is eternal.” The Padma Purana confirms: nama-cintamanih krsnas caitanya- rasa-vigrahah, purnah suddho nitya-mukto’bhinnatvan nama- naminoh, “The Holy Name is Krsna Himself, a philosopher’s stone, a form of consciousness and rasa. It is complete, pure, and eternally free from the illusory energy, for there is no difference between the name and the named.” Therefore, the Holy Names have potency, just as the named has. As corroborated in the Visnu Purana ([6.8.19](https://vedarama.com/en/books/vp/6.8.19)): avasenapi yan- namni kirtite sarva-patakaih, puman vimucyate sadyah sirhha- trastair mrgair iva, “When Lord Hari’s name is spoken even unintentionally, all sins that a person has committed disappear immediately, just like deers terrified by a lion flee away.” It is also confirmed in the Srlmad-Bhagavatam ([6.2.7](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-6/2.7)): ayam hi krta-nirveso janma-koty-amhasam api, yad vyajahara vivaso nama svasty-ayanam hareh, “Although involuntarily, because Ajamila has uttered Lord Hari’s name, which is a means of liberation, he has atoned for sins committed for millions of lifetimes.” When the scriptures state that liberation is attained by pilgrimage to holy places, austerities, and meditation, which are all difficult to perform, that applies to those who are ignorant of the power of the Holy Name. Based on the following words of the Maha-bharata, some say that the greatness of reciting the Holy Name is due to its practice being devoid of violence: japas tu sarva-dharmebhyah paramo dharma ucyate, ahirhsaya ca bhutanam japa-yajnah pravartate, “Japa is said to be the greatest of all kinds of dharma. Such japa sacrifice is performed without violence towards living beings.” This also should be understood as a repetition of words spoken by those who are ignorant of the power of the Holy Name.

### Text 9

*paramam yo mahat tejah paramam yo mahat tapah*  
*paramam yo mahad brahma paramam yah parayanam*

**Перевод:**

**He is the Supreme Person, the supreme greatness, the supreme radiance, the great ruler, the supreme power, the Supreme Brahman, and the supreme shelter of all beings.**

**Комментарий:**

Here Bhisma gives the answer to the second question. He is the Supreme Person, the supreme (paramam) greatness (mahat), i.e., He is the worshipable essence on account of His supremacy. He is the supreme radiance (tejah), the essence that gives radiance to all luminous bodies starting with the sun. He is the great ruler (tapah), the essence that rules over all such luminous bodies, as stated in the sruti: yad-bhisa vatah pavate bhisodeti suryah, bhisasmad agnis candras ca mrtyur dhavati pancamah (Taittiriya Upanisad, [2.8](https://vedarama.com/en/books/taittiriya/2.8)), “It is out of fear of Him that the wind blows. It is out of fear of Him that the sun rises. It is out of fear of Him that fire burns, the moon shines, and the fifth one, death, runs towards all creatures.” He is the supreme power (mahat), that means the omnipotent essence. He is the Supreme Brahman and the essence that constitutes the supreme shelter (parayanam) of all beings.

### Text 10

*pavitranam pavitram yo marigalanam ca mangalam*  
*daivatam devatanam ca bhutanam yo’vyayah pita*

**Перевод:**

**He is the supreme purifier among the purifying, the supreme auspicious among the auspicious, the Supreme God among divine beings, and the imperishable father and protector of all living entities.**

**Комментарий:**

Looking in retrospect, just as a lion glances backwards (simhavalokena) while moving forward, Bhisma now gives the answer to the first question. The word paramam (supreme) is also supplied here from the previous verse. Among the purifying (pavitranam), the holy places that remove obstacles, such as the River Ganga, the Supreme Lord is the supreme purifying essence (pavitram), that which imparts the power to remove sins. Among the auspicious (mangalanam), those able to remove obstacles, such as Ganesa, the Supreme Lord is the supreme auspicious (mangalam), the essence that imparts the power to remove obstacles. Among divine beings (devatanam), those who are worshipable for all, such as Brahma, the Supreme Lord is the Supreme God (daivatam), the worshipable essence on account of His supremacy. The meaning is that it is by their relationship with the Supreme Lord and by worshipping Him that the demigods are auspicious and worshipable. He is the father and protector (pita) of all living entities. The word avyayah (imperishable) indicates His distinction from the fathers of the present time.

### Text 11

*yatah sarvani bhutani bhavanty adi-yugagame*  
*yasmims ca pralayam yanti punar eva yuga-ksaye*

**Перевод:**

**All creatures are bom from Him at the time of creation, are maintained by Him in the intermediate stage, and again wind up within Him at the time of destruction.**

**Комментарий:**

Here Bhisma describes the uncommon attributes of Lord Visnu. The compound adi-yugagame means “at the time of creation,” and yuga-ksaye means “at the time of destruction.” By the word ca (and) it is expressed that during the intermediate stage, all creatures are maintained by Him. The venerable Vyasadeva, the compiler of the Brahma-sutra, declares that to be the cause of the creation, maintenance, and destruction of the universe is a characteristic of God: janmady asya yatah (1.1.1), “The Supreme Lord is the cause of the creation, maintenance and destruction of the material universe.”

### Text 12

*tasya loka-pradhanasya jagan-nathasya bhupate*  
*visnor nama-sahasram me sriu papa-bhayapaham*

**Перевод:**

**Lord Visnu, the one to be petitioned by all inhabitants of the universe, is defined in the scriptures as the Lord of all. О King, now hear from me His thousand names, which remove all sin and fear.**

**Комментарий:**

“O King (bhupate) Yudhisthira, now hear (srnu) from me the thousand names (nama-sahasram) of Lord Visnu, Who has the mentioned characteristics (tasya).” What are His names like? Bhisma says: “May the Holy Names remove (apaham) all sin (papa) and the fear (bhaya) caused by it.” This is to be expected. The affix da is used in the word apaham according to the following aphorism: asisi hanah (Astadhyayi, 3.2.49), “The affix da is used after the verbal root han\[a\] (to remove/ kill) when compounded with an object, and when wishes are intended.” What is Lord Visnu like? Bhisma says: He is defined in the scriptures (loke), such as the Vedas, as the Lord of all (pradhanasya). Therefore, He is the one to be petitioned (nathasya) by all inhabitants of the universe (jagat), starting with the demigods and human beings, as He is the giver of all goals of life (dharma, artha, kama, and moksa).

### Text 13

*yani namani gaunani vikhyatani mahatmanah*  
*rsibhih parigitani tani vaksyami bhutaye*

**Перевод:**

**I shall now speak those most well-known names of the Supreme Soul that pertain to His qualities as they have been told by the Vedic hymns and sages for the attainment of the four goals of life.**

**Комментарий:**

In order to convey the idea that it is not possible to fully narrate Lord Visnu’s names, Bhisma speaks the present verse. The word gaunani means “which ensue from qualities.” This also implies His activities. The affix an is used here according to the following aphorism of the Astadhyayi (4.3.74): tata agatah, “A taddhita affix follows the ablative case in the sense of ‘having come from.’” The meaning is that Lord Visnu’s names have His qualities and activities as semantic causes (pravrtti- nimitta) and are not mere names like words such as Dittha Among the names of the Supreme Soul (mahatmanah), Lord Visnu, those (yani) that are very well-known (vikhyatani) have been spoken of (parigitani) as transcendental by the Vedic hymns, as well as the sages (rsibhih) who see them, in order to yield the attainment of the four goals of life (bhutaye). This should be understood here— Being expressed by the word Parabrahma, Sri Krsna, the Supreme Lord Himself, is manifest in beginningless, eternal forms such as Narayana and Vasudeva, just like a vaidurya gem exhibits multiple colours. As stated in the sruti: eko vasi sarvagah krsna idya eko’pi san bahudha yo’vabhati (Gopala-tapani Upanisad, [1.20](https://vedarama.com/en/books/gopala-tapani/1.20)), “Krsna is the only all-pervasive and worshipable Supreme Lord. Although one, He appears as many.” The smrti also corroborates: krsnas tu bhagavan svayam (Srimad-Bhagavatam, [1.3.28](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-1/3.28)), “Krsna, however, is the Supreme Lord Himself.” The original form of the Lord Himself (svayaih-rupa) manifests all potencies. Lord Narayana is an expansion (vilasa) almost equal to Lord Krsna in the manifestation of potencies. The quadruple expansions (vyuha) do not manifest all potencies, and They are called plenary portions (svamsa) and portions of a plenary portion (kala). Vasudeva does not touch material nature. Sahkarsana, Pradyumna, and Aniruddha are the three purusavataras. They are the maintainers of material nature and reside respectively in the oceans called Karanoda, Garbhoda, and Ksiroda. From Garbhodasaya Visnu Himself emanate lilavataras such as Matsya and Kurma, as also the manvantaravataras and the yugavataras well known in the Puranas. The truth is that Lord Krsna is the soul and totality of all avataras. It was due to a boon received from Him that the learned Bhisma taught His names, which pertain to His particular forms distinguished by Their respective abodes and associates. Through clear meanings, Bhisma spoke names based on openly manifest activities, and through confidential meanings, he spoke names based on confidential activities. Names such as Brahma, whose primary meaning by common etymology applies to the four-headed demigod and so on, are here applied to the Supreme Lord in their meta-primary meaning by meta-etymology (maha- yoga). For instance, the primary meaning of words such as bhupa (ruler) applies to mayors and governors by common etymology, and their meta-primary meaning applies to kings and emperors by meta-etymology. Certain words whose conventional meaning is applied somewhere else are used in the sense of Lord Hari according to the conventional meaning given by learned scholars (vidvad-nidhi). The etymology of His names is also twofold: according to grammar (vaiyakarani), and according to the usage of the sages (arsi). There are also other etymological explanations besides those given here. The etymological explanations of some names that are found in the Maha-bharata and other scriptures are also to be taken into consideration. They have not been included here due to the apprehension that this treatise may become too voluminous. Although some names, such as Visnu, have been repeated, there is no tautology (punarukti), as each instance bears a different meaning. Although names such as Sridhara and Madhava have the same meaning, there is no tautology, since, they belong to different forms. The repetition of meanings is also not a fault, as all names have for their object one and the same deity under question. Here ends the introduction.

### Text 14

*om visvam visnur vasat-karo bhuta-bhavya-bhavat-prabhuh*  
*bhuta-krd bhuta-bhrd bhavo bhutatma bhuta-bhavanah*

**Комментарий:**

Here Bhisma speaks some names that indicate attributes of completeness and supreme potency, since they increase one’s eagerness for Lord Visnu, the possessor of these attributes. As this hymn starts and ends with the pranava, which is the Supreme Lord Himself, it has the status of mantra. By the word namah at the end with each name in the dative case, it is also indicated that by this hymn, Lord Visnu’s purascarana (preliminary rite) is performed in the form of salutations accompanied by the offering of tulasi leaves. Ihe forms of words such as visva should be understood as visvaya in the dative case because here they are names rather than pronouns. The names here are read in the feminine, masculine, and neuter genders, and it should be understood that the words devata, deva, and daiva are to be respectively qualified by them. (1) **Visva**— He Who enters (visati) within all elements different from Himself. This word is formed by adding the affix kvanip after the verbal root vis\[a\] (to enter). (2)**Visnu**— He Who pervades all those elements. This norm is formed with the affix nu, which is called a kit affix, in conformity with the following aphorism: viseh kic ca (Paniniya Unadi-sutra, 3.39), “After the verbal root vis\[l\] (to pervade), the affix nu is used, which is a kit affix.” (3)**Vasat-kara**— He unto Whom the exclamation vasat is made (karah), that means He unto Whom oblations are offered, the One to be worshipped in all sacrifices. By bahula the affix ghan is used here denoting location, just like the word pratyahara. That is because He is (4)**Bhuta- bhavya-bhavat-prabhu**, the Lord (prabhu) of all beings that existed (bhuta), exist (bhavat), and shall exist (bhavya), that means the Master of all beings that belong to the past, present, and future. According to some, this name means that He is the controller (prabhu) of threefold time: past, present, and future. (5)**Bhuta-krt**— He Who creates (krt) the great material elements (bhuta) starting with ether. (6)**Bhuta-bhrt**— He Who maintains (bhrt) those elements (bhuta), Who supports them by His mere will. (7)**Bhava**— He Who, in the form of the material manifestation, becomes endowed with sentient and insentient energies. According to the Kasika-vrtti on the Astadhyayi (3.1.143), the affix na is also used after the verb bhu (to become). As stated in the sruti: so’kamayata bahu syarn prajayeya (Taittiriya Upanisad, [2.6.1](https://vedarama.com/en/books/taittiriya/2.6.1)), “The Supreme Soul desired, ‘Let Me become many.” He is called (8)**Bhutatma**, the soul (atma) of all beings (bhuta), because by His personal expansion, He is the originator of all sentient and insentient beings. (9)**Bhuta-bhavana**— He Who, by His potency and opulence, manifests (bhavana) those beings (bhuta).

### Text 15

*putatma paramatma ca muktanam parama gatih*  
*avyayah purusah saksI ksetra-jno’ksara eva ca*

**Комментарий:**

(10)**Putatma**— He Whose soul (atma) is free from contact (pitta) with the material elements, although being their creator. (11)**Paramatma**— He Whose self (atma) is everlasting fortune (parama) itself. (12)**Muktanam parama gatih**— He is the ultimate (parama) aim (gatih) for the living entities who are eternally free (muktanam) from ignorance and those who become free from ignorance by some means. Ardent desire for Lord Visnu’s svarupa, in comparison to His power, is indicated by the adjective parama. The theory that there is only one soul is herein refuted by the mention of the liberated souls as different from one another and from the Supreme Lord. (13)**Avyaya**— He Who does not deviate (na vyeti) from His affection towards the liberated souls. The affix ac is used here according to the rule that it follows a group of verbs starting with рас (to cook). (14)**Purusa**— He Who gives (sanoti) abundant rewards (puru) to the liberated souls. The affix da is used here in conformity with the following aphorism: anyesv api drsyate (Astadhyayi, 3.2.101), “The affix da is also seen with other words (and verbal roots).” (15)**Saksi**— He Who directly observes everything as if situated in the palm of His hand. (16) **Ksetra-jna**— He Who simultaneously knows (jna) the bodies (ksetra) of all living entities. As stated in the smrti.- ksetrani hi sarirani bijam capi subhasubhe, tani vetti sa yogatma tatah ksetra-jna ucyate (Maha-bharata, 12.339.6), “The Supreme Person, Who is the essence of yoga, knows the bodies of all living entities as well as the pious and sinful activities that are the cause of those bodies. Therefore, He is called ksetrajna, the knower of the field.” (17)**Aksara**— He Who is never deprived (na ksarati) of His original form and nature.

### Text 16

*yogo yoga-vidam neta pradhana-purusesvarah*  
*nara-simha-vapuh sriman kesavah purusottamah*

**Комментарий:**

(18)**Yoga**— He upon Whom the mind is fixed (yujyate), the auspicious resting-place of meditation (samadhi). The affix ghan is used here according to the aphorism ‘halas ca’ (Astadhyayi, 3.3.121), which is governed by the aphorism ‘akartari ca karake samjnayam’ (ibid., 3.3.19). (19)**Yoga- vidam neta**— the leader (neta) of those who deliberate (vindate) on yoga, the cessation of the activities of the mind, i.e., He Who leads to yoga. (20)**Pradhana-purusesvara**— He Who rules (iste) over material nature (pradhana) and the living entities (purusa). (21)**Nara-simha-vapu**— He Whose body (vapu) is human (nara) and lion (sirhha), Whose divine form is that of a man-lion for the protection of His devotee. Although He manifests such an extraordinary and terrifying form of a man and a lion, which are mutually difficult to be accomplished in a single body, He is (22)**Sriman**, He Whose beauty is exceedingly charming. According to the Visva-kosa (Ra, 2), the word srl is used in the sense of dressing style and beauty. (23)**Kesava**— the progenitor of Brahma (Ka) and Rudra (Isa). As stated by Rudra: ka iti brahmano nama iso’ham sarva- dehinam, avam tavanga-sambhutau tasmat kesava-nama-bhak (Hari-vamsa, 3.88.48), “Ka is the name of Brahma, and I am Isa, the master of all embodied beings. Both of us were born from Your body, and therefore You are named Kesava.” He is named (24)**Purusottama** on account of His most exalted position (uttama) among all conditioned and liberated living entities (purusa), as confirmed by the smrti: yasmat ksaram atito’ham aksarad api cottamah, ato’smi loke vede ca prathitah purusottamah (Bhagavad-gita, [15.18](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/15.18)), “Because I am situated beyond the conditioned souls and even higher than the liberated souls, I am known in the Vedas and the smrti as the Supreme Person.”

### Text 17

*sarvah sarvah sivah sthanur bhutadir nidhir avyayah*  
*sambhavo bhavano bharta prabhavah prabhur isvarah*

**Комментарий:**

(25)**Sarva**— He Who moves (sarati), pervades. The affix van is applied after the verb sr (to move). As stated by Arjuna: sarvarii samapnosi tato’si sarvah (Bhagavad-gita, [11.40](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/11.40)), “You pervade everything, and therefore You are everything.” (26) **Sarva**— He Who, upon being remembered, destroys (srnati) inauspiciousness. The formation of this noun is similar to the above one, but the affix used isva. Therefore, He is (27**)Siva**, aupisciousness personified. The affix van is applied after the verbal root si\[n\] (to be auspicious). By irregular formation (nipatana), the long i becomes short i and there is no guna. It has been previously said that Lord Visnu is the supreme auspicious among the auspicious. He is called (28)**Sthanu** (stable) because He is steady in His compassion. Therefore, He is named (29)**Bhiitadi**, He is accepted (adiyate) by the living entities (bhuta) as the giver of auspiciousness. This word is formed according to the following aphorism: upasarge ghoh kih (Astadhyayi, 3.3.92), “The affix ki is used after ghu verbs accompanied by a preposition.” (30)**Nidhir avyayah**— He is placed within (nidhiyate) the heart just like an invaluable jewel. He is inexhaustible (avyayah), for despite being eternally depended upon by all living beings, He does not diminish even a farthing. The word avyayah is an adjective of the word nidhih (treasure). (31)**Sambhava**— He Whose manifestation (bhava) in forms such as Matsya for the sake of the protection of the virtuous is accomplished by His thoroughly (sam) unswerving svarupa and nature. As it is said in the smrti: paritranaya sadhunarh vinasaya ca duskrtam, dharma-samsthapanarthaya sambhavami yuge yuge (Bhagavad-gita, [4.8](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/4.8)), “To deliver the pious and to annihilate the miscreants, as well as to re-establish the principles of religion, I Myself appear, millennium after millennium.” (32)**Bhavana**— He Who reflects (bhavayati), “I shall protect the devotees in such and such a way.” As when Lord Visnu killed Hiranyakasipu after searching for a loophole, although the latter had received a boon that was flawless, and also in the way He gave prosperity to the Pandavas by various means. (33)**Bharta**— He Who maintains (bibharti) the devotees by giving them His Own bliss. He is called (34)**Prabhava** because the exalted (prakrsta) manifestation (bhava) of His devotees’ own spiritual form is due to Him. As stated in the sruti; sa esa samprasado’smac charirat samutthaya param jyotir upasampadya svena riipenabhinispadyate (Chandogya Upanisad, [8.12.3](https://vedarama.com/en/books/chan-upa/8.12.3)), “Becoming thus liberated, the individual soul rises out from the body, and having attained the Supreme Lord, becomes manifest in his own spiritual form.” He is (35) **Prabhu**, the Lord, for although descending in forms similar to humans and animals bound by the law of karma, He performs activities that are impossible for demigods and other higher beings. Just as when He brought back the son of His guru, Sandipani Muni, and the sons of a brahmana in Dvaraka; when He revived Pariksit, who had been burnt by a brahmastra, when He awarded entrance in the heavenly planets to wicked individuals like Ghantakarna and Jara; and when He gave liberation to those who died in the war of Kuruksetra. He is known as (36)**Isvara**, the Ruler, for even in the guise of a human and so on, He rules over Brahma, Siva, and everyone else. As stated by Siva: isas tvam sarva-bhutanam isvaro’si sada smrtah (Hari-varhsa, 3.88.54), “You are always described in the scriptures as God, the Ruler of all living entities.”

### Text 18

*svayambhuh sambhur adityah puskarakso maha-svanah*  
*anadi-nidhano dhata vidhata dhatur uttamah*

**Комментарий:**

(37)**Svayambhu**— He Who manifests (bhu) Himself by His Own will (svayam). The affix kvip is used here according to the following aphorism: bhuvah sarhjnantarayoh (Astadhyayi, 3.2.179), “The affix kvip follows the verbal root bhu when a name or an intermediate is denoted.” It is also said in the words of the Vedic mantras: kavir manisi paribhuh svayambhuh (Isa Upanisad, [8](https://vedarama.com/en/books/iso/8)), “The Supreme Lord is omniscient, wise, all­pervading, and self-manifest.” (38)**Sambhu**— He Who causes (bhavayati) happiness (sam) by revealing His auspicious qualities. The affix du is used here, since this noun belongs to a group of words beginning with mitadru. (39)**Aditya**— He Whose residence is the sun. The affix nya is used with names such as Diti, Aditi, and Aditya. As stated in the sruti: ya eso’ntar aditye hiran-mayah puruso drsyate (Chandogya Upanisad, [1.6.6](https://vedarama.com/en/books/chan-upa/part-1.chapter-6.166)), “That person within the sun is perceived as having a golden form.” (40)**Puskaraksa**— He Who pervades (aksnoti) the sky (puskara). According to the Amara-kosa (1.2.1), the words vyoma, puskara, and ambara are synonyms of “sky.” It is also said in the sruti: akasatma (Chandogya Upanisad, [3.14.2](https://vedarama.com/en/books/chan-upa/part-3.chapter-14.3142-3)), “The Supreme Soul’s nature is all-pervasive like the sky.” (41)**Maha-svana**— He Whose sound (svana) in the form of the Vedas is worshipable (mahan). As stated in the sruti: tasya va etasya mahato bhutasya nisvasitam etad yad rg- vedo yajur-vedah (Brhad-aranyaka Upanisad, [2.4.10](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bau/2.4.10)), “The four Vedas are the exhalation of the Supreme Being.” He is called (42)**Anadi-nidhana** because He is devoid of origination (adi) and destruction (nidhana).
